### 252109899e — 001_trend_breakout_trail (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:09 UTC · idea `ideas/001_trend_breakout_trail.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A 15m breakout that agrees with the higher-timeframe trend is a continuation setup whose counterparty is the trapped breakout trader on the failed break of the opposite side. Those traps get filled on the retest, so the move continues. Two things decide whether this pays: (a) the stop must be wide (4-5x ATR) so that cost_r = 0.14%/stop% falls to roughly 0.06-0.08 R, because a continuation edge is small in absolute price terms; (b) there must be NO fixed take-profit, because the example idea's 3R cap only reached 7% while 61% of trades expired on time - the payoff of a trend entry is entirely in the few trades that run, and a cap throws them away.
- grid: 12 combos, 12 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### 5066a68b70 — 002_pct_stop_trend_breakout (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:09 UTC · idea `ideas/002_pct_stop_trend_breakout.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 001 measured a real gross edge on 15m Donchian breakouts (+0.125 R train, +0.155 R valid) and still lost, because an ATR-multiple stop is not a constant price distance: the same 3.0x ATR was 1.28% of price in 2020-2022 but 0.78% in 2023-2024, so cost_r rose from 0.109 to 0.179 and exceeded the gross edge. The counterparty and the continuation behaviour are unchanged; only the cost accounting was regime-dependent. Fixing the stop at a fraction of PRICE makes cost_r a constant 0.14%/pct, independent of the volatility regime, which is the single change that decides whether this gross edge survives. This idea therefore tests one mechanism, not a parameter search: a 1.5% stop costs 0.093 R whether ATR is 0.3% or 1.0% of price.

### 55b321947e — 003_funding_fade (BTCUSDT 15m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 00:09 UTC · idea `ideas/003_funding_fade.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Extreme funding is a crowding measurement, not a signal by itself: a positive rate means longs are paying to hold, so the crowd is long and its exit is forced by cost rather than by conviction. When that crowded funding coincides with a price that is stretched far from its own recent mean, the stretched side is the one paying, and the reversion is caused by the crowd unwinding against itself. Requiring BOTH conditions (extreme funding AND a stretched z-score, within 8 bars of each other) should separate a real crowded unwind from ordinary drift.

### d3042109b4 — 004_squeeze_expansion (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:09 UTC · idea `ideas/004_squeeze_expansion.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A Bollinger-width squeeze means liquidity providers have widened their quotes and the breakout traders who sold the compression are now flat or stopped, so the market is thin of both sides. The first expansion bar out of that thin book, if it agrees with the higher-timeframe trend, is a genuine regime change rather than noise: the counterparty is the crowd that bought the bottom of the range and is now short into the expansion. Requiring the ATR ratio to be above 1 at entry ensures we are entering on the expansion, not during the compression.

### cdde91ae48 — 005_session_open_break (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:09 UTC · idea `ideas/005_session_open_break.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The London and New York opens are when the day's concentrated flow arrives: desks that must trade the session open cross the spread, and the overnight range is the agreed reference price they cross. A break of the pre-session range with genuine volume is therefore filled by real flow instead of by an opposing taker, unlike a mid-session breakout. The post-only entry is part of the hypothesis, not a saving: at the range edge we can rest a limit and pay maker instead of taker, which is worth 0.03% of price on this idea's wider stop.
- grid: 16 combos, 16 with ≥100 train trades, 94% of those positive on train

### 0269bf5d77 — 008_short_breakout_exit_by_price (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:09 UTC · idea `ideas/008_short_breakout_exit_by_price.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 007's short-side Donchian with a 2% stop is profitable on both periods (+0.049 train, +0.070 valid) but 82% of its trades close on the 8-hour time stop and only 18% on the stop: the clock, not price, is ending the average trade, so its gross_r of +0.156 is really a measurement of 8-hour BTC drift rather than of the breakout. Exp 009 independently found the marginal edge in this project needs 6-18h holds, which is the same statement. If the time stop is what truncates winners, then (a) removing the clock with a long hold and (b) arming the ATR trail early enough to actually trigger (at 1.5R the price must move 3% inside 8h, which is rare) should both raise gross_r without touching the entry or the cost side of the ledger.

### 32e903fb0d — 009_short_breakout_5m_pct_stop (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/009_short_breakout_5m_pct_stop.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 007/008 fail on exactly one gate: 233 validation trades give a 95% CI of [-0.041, +0.165] around a mean R of +0.070, so the estimate is too imprecise, not negative. In every earlier experiment of this project a lower timeframe lost, and the reason was always cost_r: the same round trip costs more R when the stop is narrow. That reason no longer applies, because idea 002's percentage-of-price stop makes cost_r = 0.14%/2% = 0.07 R independent of the timeframe. A 5m version of the same setup should therefore keep the same cost_r, produce roughly three times the signals, and shrink the confidence interval by about sqrt(3) without needing a better edge. Every lookback is scaled by 3x (Donchian 48->144, ADX 14->42, ATR 14->42, HTF 50->150, cooldown 4->12) so that every indicator covers the same span of TIME and the only variable that changes is how finely the market is sampled.

### 70fb497bcf — 010_short_breakout_post_only (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/010_short_breakout_post_only.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Exp 008/010 measured that a post-only entry cuts the round trip from 0.14% to 0.09% of price because a stop-loss never receives a maker fill, only the entry does - a measured 1.55x, not an assumption. Idea 007's short-only breakout pays cost_r 0.086 against a gross edge of +0.156, so execution is 55% of its gross edge and the single largest remaining item on the ledger. This idea holds the signal, stop, direction and exit fixed at idea 007's train-selected values and changes only HOW the entry is filled, gridding the limit offset. The cost is a lower fill rate: a post-only limit is only filled when price comes back to the level, which is adverse selection, and the net effect must be measured rather than assumed.

### 29b6b39400 — 011_short_breakout_wider_stop (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/011_short_breakout_wider_stop.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: In every run of the post-only short breakout (ideas 007, 008, 010) the grid selection landed on stop.pct = 0.02, which was the WIDEST value offered - the optimum is sitting on the edge of the grid, not inside it. That is a diagnosable condition, not a reason to keep tuning: if cost_r = round_trip_cost / stop_pct is the mechanism, the selection must keep moving outward until gross_r starts falling faster than cost_r does. This idea extends the grid outward to 3.0% and adds a fourth level so the optimum can be found rather than pinned at the boundary, and keeps the post-only execution of idea 010 because the mechanism being tested is the same one in both regimes. Prediction if the mechanism is right: cost_r falls monotonically with pct and mean R peaks at an interior value instead of at the last one.

### 72454823e9 — 012_short_breakout_taker_flow (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/012_short_breakout_taker_flow.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 010's short breakout wins 64% of its trades with 82% of them closed by the clock rather than by the stop or the trail, which means its edge has not yet been explained: it could be drift, or it could be a real distribution imbalance that the Donchian break only marks. The taker buy ratio measures which side is crossing the spread aggressively, and it is the one piece of order-flow information available in the raw klines. A downward break that aggressive sellers are driving should continue, because the counterparty is the resting bid that will be pulled; a downward break on buyer aggression is a pullback that should fail. Requiring taker_flow to agree with the short side therefore tests whether the edge is order flow or merely direction, and if it is order flow the surviving trades should be better, not just fewer.
- grid: 8 combos, 6 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### 7bcd1ac0d7 — 013_squeeze_expansion_pct_stop (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/013_squeeze_expansion_pct_stop.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 004 measured the largest gross edge found anywhere in this project - +0.275 R on train and +0.269 R on valid, roughly double every other entry tested - and still returned REJECT, because it also measured the largest cost: cost_r 0.205. The diagnosis is mechanical. A squeeze filter deliberately selects the calmest periods in the sample, so ATR is at its minimum exactly when the trade is taken, and a 3.0x ATR stop placed there is only ~0.7% of price. That narrow stop both doubles cost_r and stops out 71% of the trades, while the ATR stop that behaves well in idea 010 (calmer ATR, 18% stop-outs) is unusable here. A percentage-of-price stop is regime-independent by construction, so it should keep cost_r near 0.07 and let the expansion trade run past the noise that a volatility-scaled stop cannot distinguish from a real reversal. The squeeze edge is the most valuable thing measured so far; this idea tests whether it was ever affordable.

### 942d22a978 — 014_short_breakout_funding_crowding (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/014_short_breakout_funding_crowding.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 010's short breakout wins 64% of its trades and we still cannot say why, which means we cannot tell whether it will survive the next regime. There is one mechanism that would make SHORT breakouts specifically work rather than breakouts in general: crowding. A leveraged long crowd pays funding, so it is structurally short of patience; when price makes a new 12-hour low that crowd is liquidated and its forced selling is what extends the move. Under that mechanism the edge must be concentrated where longs are the crowded side - positive funding - and must disappear when funding is negative and the crowd is already short. This idea therefore blocks the short side when funding is extreme on the short side, which is the only condition under which the crowding story predicts the trade should NOT be taken. If mean R rises materially, the edge has a reason and a defined population; if it is unchanged, idea 010 is unexplained drift and should not be trusted.
- grid: 8 combos, 8 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### 785b9ba3b1 — 015_short_supertrend_robustness (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/015_short_supertrend_robustness.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Two ideas in a row (012 taker_flow, 014 funding crowding) returned results identical to idea 010 to the last decimal, because the train selection chose filter thresholds so loose that they blocked nothing. That pattern is itself the finding: the edge does not live in any filter, it lives in being short while the higher-timeframe trend is down. If that is true, it should not depend on HOW the short entry is detected. Supertrend is a completely different mechanism from a Donchian channel - it is an ATR trailing stop that flips, not a price channel that breaks - so it shares no parameter with idea 010 except direction, trend filter and stop width. If it reproduces the same mean R, the effect is a property of shorting BTC in a downtrend and is as likely to appear in a different sample as this one. If it returns nothing, then idea 010 is one narrow pattern measured 219 times, and a mean R of +0.085 on 219 trades is exactly the kind of number that a holdout exists to destroy.

### dc08ab8828 — 016_long_mean_reversion_pct_stop (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:10 UTC · idea `ideas/016_long_mean_reversion_pct_stop.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long-side idea in this project has been a continuation idea, and every continuation idea has lost; every idea that showed gross_r worth having was short. The untested corner is the opposite trade: after BTC has been pushed away from its own recent mean, does it come back? The counterparty is specific and identifiable - the traders who bought the extension are underwater, and a bounce is the price at which they can exit without admitting the loss, so their selling caps the bounce and the move reverts. Crucially this is the one idea where a percentage-of-price stop makes sense on its own terms rather than as a cost hack: a reversion has a known destination (the mean), so the stop is placed below the stretch, not at a volatility multiple. The old range-reversion example failed on train, but it used a swing stop clamped to 3 ATR minimum, so its risk was never controlled. This tests the only long-side mechanism left, with the cost side fixed.

### 7bb5be83b1 — 020_failed_break_fade (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:24 UTC · idea `ideas\020_failed_break_fade.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 measured why shorting breakouts lost: on idea 010's 339 validation shorts, price rose against the position by at least 0.25R in 75% of trades and by 0.5R in 47%, with a mean adverse excursion of +0.57R reached in a median of 10 bars. The break below the 12-hour low was therefore usually a FAILED breakdown - the sellers who sold it are trapped below the level and have to cover, which is exactly the pressure that pushes price back up. So the trade to take is not the break but its failure: once price has closed back inside the n-bar range within n_bars bars, go long, and the mirror case for failed breakouts. This is a new Level 2 trigger, causal, and deliberately carries no regime filter: a failed breakdown is by definition a trade against the recent move, so requiring the move to still look like a trend would remove most of the population.
- grid: 12 combos, 12 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### 0b7488d90e — 017_session_open_long_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:25 UTC · idea `ideas\017_session_open_long_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The London and New York opens are when the day's concentrated order flow arrives: desks that must trade the session open cross the spread, and the pre-session range is the agreed reference price they cross. R1.0 measured that idea 005's edge was entirely its long leg (+0.1275 R on 212 validation trades, gross +0.3088, positive in both 2023 and 2024) while its short leg was a coin flip, so this idea takes the session break long only. It also fixes what 005 could not afford: 005 paid cost_r 0.1813 because a 2.5x ATR stop on 15m is under 1% of price and was crossed as taker, so the stop here is a fixed fraction of price and the entry is a post-only limit. ### 1e1149a0c2 — 018_trend_breakout_long_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 00:25 UTC · idea `ideas\018_trend_breakout_long_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 showed that the long breakout's expectancy is flat across every regime - by BTC's 30-day trend at entry it was -0.026 in a bear market and +0.025 in a strong bull, with corr(R, trend) = +0.011 - so this is not a disguised long-a-bull-market bet, which is the usual way a long-only idea dies. The mechanism being claimed is narrower: a break of the n-bar high taken in the direction of the higher-timeframe trend, in a market ADX calls trending, is continuation because the counterparty is the seller who is late to a rising market. Direction is fixed long on purpose, because when the grid was allowed to choose, TRAIN chose long and the short leg lost significantly (Exp 015). ### ca0838da05 — 019_pullback_uptrend_long_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 00:25 UTC · idea `ideas\019_pullback_uptrend_long_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long idea in this project so far has paid up at a breakout, and R1.0 showed the only measured long edge in it - 005's long leg, gross +0.31 R - came from a session range break. This idea tests the other way to get long exposure: in an established uptrend, price dips to the fast EMA and closes back above it, where the counterparty is the seller who is late to a rising market and is selling into a dip that trend-following money wants. The dip is where size is available, rather than where the breakout crowd pays up, so the entry should be cheaper in R terms even if it fills less often. Direction is long because the long side is the only one that has ever shown a gross edge on this market (Exp 015). [Timeframe variant of 019_pullback_uptrend_long, 15m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x2, pct stop x1.414.]

### 85da7e2f2f — 020_failed_break_fade_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:25 UTC · idea `ideas\020_failed_break_fade_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 measured why shorting breakouts lost: on idea 010's 339 validation shorts, price rose against the position by at least 0.25R in 75% of trades and by 0.5R in 47%, with a mean adverse excursion of +0.57R reached in a median of 10 bars. The break below the 12-hour low was therefore usually a FAILED breakdown - the sellers who sold it are trapped below the level and have to cover, which is exactly the pressure that pushes price back up. So the trade to take is not the break but its failure: once price has closed back inside the n-bar range within n_bars bars, go long, and the mirror case for failed breakouts. This is a new Level 2 trigger, causal, and deliberately carries no regime filter: a failed breakdown is by definition a trade against the recent move, so requiring the move to still look like a trend would remove most of the population. [Timeframe variant of 020_failed_break_fade, 15m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x2, pct stop x1.414.]

### 92e2c3808b — 020_failed_break_fade_tf60 (BTCUSDT 60m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:25 UTC · idea `ideas\020_failed_break_fade_tf60.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 measured why shorting breakouts lost: on idea 010's 339 validation shorts, price rose against the position by at least 0.25R in 75% of trades and by 0.5R in 47%, with a mean adverse excursion of +0.57R reached in a median of 10 bars. The break below the 12-hour low was therefore usually a FAILED breakdown - the sellers who sold it are trapped below the level and have to cover, which is exactly the pressure that pushes price back up. So the trade to take is not the break but its failure: once price has closed back inside the n-bar range within n_bars bars, go long, and the mirror case for failed breakouts. This is a new Level 2 trigger, causal, and deliberately carries no regime filter: a failed breakdown is by definition a trade against the recent move, so requiring the move to still look like a trend would remove most of the population. [Timeframe variant of 020_failed_break_fade, 15m -> 60m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x4, pct stop x2.000.]

### 842acaab55 — 006_long_only_trend_tf60 (BTCUSDT 60m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 00:25 UTC · idea `ideas\006_long_only_trend_tf60.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: On a 15-30m timeframe the short side of BTC carries no drift: it is pure cost. A long-only version of the same trend entry keeps the identical trigger and exit, so any difference in mean R between this idea and a both-sides version of the same idea measures how much the short side costs. If long-only is materially better, the negative results in this project are a statement about short intraday BTC, not about the trigger. ### 42f3955f81 — 005_session_open_break_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:26 UTC · idea `ideas\005_session_open_break_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The London and New York opens are when the day's concentrated flow arrives: desks that must trade the session open cross the spread, and the overnight range is the agreed reference price they cross. A break of the pre-session range with genuine volume is therefore filled by real flow instead of by an opposing taker, unlike a mid-session breakout. The post-only entry is part of the hypothesis, not a saving: at the range edge we can rest a limit and pay maker instead of taker, which is worth 0.03% of price on this idea's wider stop. [Timeframe variant of 005_session_open_break, 15m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x2, pct stop x1.414.]
- grid: 16 combos, 16 with ≥100 train trades, 44% of those positive on train

### 5e12237592 — 018_trend_breakout_long_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:26 UTC · idea `ideas\018_trend_breakout_long_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`

The mechanism being claimed is narrower: a break of the n-bar high taken in the direction of the higher-timeframe trend, in a market ADX calls trending, is continuation because the counterparty is the seller who is late to a rising market. ### 6d1604ce79 — 019_pullback_uptrend_long_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:26 UTC · idea `ideas\019_pullback_uptrend_long_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long idea in this project so far has paid up at a breakout, and R1.0 showed the only measured long edge in it - 005's long leg, gross +0.31 R - came from a session range break. This idea tests the other way to get long exposure: in an established uptrend, price dips to the fast EMA and closes back above it, where the counterparty is the seller who is late to a rising market and is selling into a dip that trend-following money wants. The dip is where size is available, rather than where the breakout crowd pays up, so the entry should be cheaper in R terms even if it fills less often. Direction is long because the long side is the only one that has ever shown a gross edge on this market (Exp 015). ### a2ed5ef95b — 020_failed_break_fade_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:27 UTC · idea `ideas\020_failed_break_fade_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 measured why shorting breakouts lost: on idea 010's 339 validation shorts, price rose against the position by at least 0.25R in 75% of trades and by 0.5R in 47%, with a mean adverse excursion of +0.57R reached in a median of 10 bars. The break below the 12-hour low was therefore usually a FAILED breakdown - the sellers who sold it are trapped below the level and have to cover, which is exactly the pressure that pushes price back up. So the trade to take is not the break but its failure: once price has closed back inside the n-bar range within n_bars bars, go long, and the mirror case for failed breakouts. This is a new Level 2 trigger, causal, and deliberately carries no regime filter: a failed breakdown is by definition a trade against the recent move, so requiring the move to still look like a trend would remove most of the population. [Timeframe variant of 020_failed_break_fade, 15m -> 5m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.333333, pct stop x0.577.]
- grid: 12 combos, 12 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### dd69631a80 — 006_long_only_trend_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:27 UTC · idea `ideas\006_long_only_trend_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: On a 15-30m timeframe the short side of BTC carries no drift: it is pure cost. A long-only version of the same trend entry keeps the identical trigger and exit, so any difference in mean R between this idea and a both-sides version of the same idea measures how much the short side costs. If long-only is materially better, the negative results in this project are a statement about short intraday BTC, not about the trigger. ### a076fd60b4 — 005_session_open_break_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:27 UTC · idea `ideas\005_session_open_break_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The London and New York opens are when the day's concentrated flow arrives: desks that must trade the session open cross the spread, and the overnight range is the agreed reference price they cross. A break of the pre-session range with genuine volume is therefore filled by real flow instead of by an opposing taker, unlike a mid-session breakout. The post-only entry is part of the hypothesis, not a saving: at the range edge we can rest a limit and pay maker instead of taker, which is worth 0.03% of price on this idea's wider stop. [Timeframe variant of 005_session_open_break, 15m -> 5m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.333333, pct stop x0.577.]

### 0cdec9746b — 017_session_open_long_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:27 UTC · idea `ideas\017_session_open_long_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The London and New York opens are when the day's concentrated order flow arrives: desks that must trade the session open cross the spread, and the pre-session range is the agreed reference price they cross. R1.0 measured that idea 005's edge was entirely its long leg (+0.1275 R on 212 validation trades, gross +0.3088, positive in both 2023 and 2024) while its short leg was a coin flip, so this idea takes the session break long only. It also fixes what 005 could not afford: 005 paid cost_r 0.1813 because a 2.5x ATR stop on 15m is under 1% of price and was crossed as taker, so the stop here is a fixed fraction of price and the entry is a post-only limit. ### e4eb691e2b — 018_trend_breakout_long_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:28 UTC · idea `ideas\018_trend_breakout_long_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 showed that the long breakout's expectancy is flat across every regime - by BTC's 30-day trend at entry it was -0.026 in a bear market and +0.025 in a strong bull, with corr(R, trend) = +0.011 - so this is not a disguised long-a-bull-market bet, which is the usual way a long-only idea dies. The mechanism being claimed is narrower: a break of the n-bar high taken in the direction of the higher-timeframe trend, in a market ADX calls trending, is continuation because the counterparty is the seller who is late to a rising market. Direction is fixed long on purpose, because when the grid was allowed to choose, TRAIN chose long and the short leg lost significantly (Exp 015). [Timeframe variant of 018_trend_breakout_long, 15m -> 3m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.2, pct stop x0.447.]

### 694458d503 — 019_pullback_uptrend_long_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:29 UTC · idea `ideas\019_pullback_uptrend_long_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long idea in this project so far has paid up at a breakout, and R1.0 showed the only measured long edge in it - 005's long leg, gross +0.31 R - came from a session range break. This idea tests the other way to get long exposure: in an established uptrend, price dips to the fast EMA and closes back above it, where the counterparty is the seller who is late to a rising market and is selling into a dip that trend-following money wants. The dip is where size is available, rather than where the breakout crowd pays up, so the entry should be cheaper in R terms even if it fills less often. Direction is long because the long side is the only one that has ever shown a gross edge on this market (Exp 015). [Timeframe variant of 019_pullback_uptrend_long, 15m -> 3m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.2, pct stop x0.447.]

### 457aafca18 — 020_failed_break_fade_tf1 (BTCUSDT 1m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:30 UTC · idea `ideas\020_failed_break_fade_tf1.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: R1.0 measured why shorting breakouts lost: on idea 010's 339 validation shorts, price rose against the position by at least 0.25R in 75% of trades and by 0.5R in 47%, with a mean adverse excursion of +0.57R reached in a median of 10 bars. The break below the 12-hour low was therefore usually a FAILED breakdown - the sellers who sold it are trapped below the level and have to cover, which is exactly the pressure that pushes price back up. So the trade to take is not the break but its failure: once price has closed back inside the n-bar range within n_bars bars, go long, and the mirror case for failed breakouts. This is a new Level 2 trigger, causal, and deliberately carries no regime filter: a failed breakdown is by definition a trade against the recent move, so requiring the move to still look like a trend would remove most of the population. [Timeframe variant of 020_failed_break_fade, 15m -> 1m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.0666667, pct stop x0.258.]
- grid: 12 combos, 12 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### f399da35db — 005_session_open_break_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 00:31 UTC · idea `ideas\005_session_open_break_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The London and New York opens are when the day's concentrated flow arrives: desks that must trade the session open cross the spread, and the overnight range is the agreed reference price they cross. A break of the pre-session range with genuine volume is therefore filled by real flow instead of by an opposing taker, unlike a mid-session breakout. The post-only entry is part of the hypothesis, not a saving: at the range edge we can rest a limit and pay maker instead of taker, which is worth 0.03% of price on this idea's wider stop. [Timeframe variant of 005_session_open_break, 15m -> 3m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.2, pct stop x0.447.]
- grid: 16 combos, 16 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### 2395a0a9bd — 021_exit_profit_side (BTCUSDT 30m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 00:54 UTC · idea `ideas\021_exit_profit_side.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Round 1's best configuration has inert exits: TP 0%, time exits 69%, average hold 20.8h, because `trail_at` 1.5R against a 2.83% stop needs price to travel 4.2% before the trail arms, and on 30m bars that almost never happens inside the hold. So its +0.102 R is 24-hour drift with a stop attached, not trade management. This study asks the profit-side question only, with the entry frozen: does a take-profit bank the winners, does break-even protect them, and how early should the trail arm? Three of the four keys switch a mechanism on or off (tp.type, be_at, trail_at) precisely because the incumbent has all of them effectively disabled, so the grid is asking which mechanism earns its keep rather than tuning a number that already works.

### 199abd9497 — 022_exit_risk_side (BTCUSDT 30m) — **PASS**

- 2026-09-29 00:55 UTC · idea `ideas\022_exit_risk_side.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The risk side of idea 018 at 30m has never been tested: its stop was fixed at 2.83% of price and its hold at 24h by the chart-mode scaling of a 15m source, not by any argument, and 69% of its trades are closed by that clock rather than by price. Three things are being asked here. (1) Stop width: a `pct` stop at 1% costs 0.09 R against it while a 3% stop costs 0.03, and the round has already shown that cost_r is the single largest term, so the optimum may sit much wider than the incumbent. (2) Stop kind: a `swing` stop sits beyond the recent structure instead of at a fixed distance, which is the only way to let the stop follow volatility without the regime-dependence an ATR multiple brings - Exp 012 measured that an ATR multiple cost 0.109 R on train and 0.179 R on valid for the same setup. (3) Hold length: if the edge really is multi-day drift, short holds should destroy it and long holds should not; if it is an 8h artefact, the opposite. The entry is frozen so TRAIN picks the risk side and VALID judges it. The stop dict carries n 16, buffer_atr 0.3, min_atr 1.5 and max_atr 8 so the `swing` branch works; max_atr is 8 rather than 6 because at 30m ATR is 0.52% of price, so a 6-ATR ceiling is 3.09% and would silently clamp the widest stop.pct in the grid - the pct branch must not be distorted by a parameter that exists for the swing branch.
- grid: 32 combos, 32 with ≥100 train trades, 47% of those positive on train
- chosen params: `{"atr_n": 14, "cooldown_bars": 4, "direction": "long", "filters": [{"mult": 4, "n": 50, "type": "htf_trend"}, {"min": 20, "n": 14, "type": "adx_min"}], "max_hold_hours": 24, "stop": {"buffer_atr": 0.3, "max_atr": 8.0, "min_atr": 1.5, "n": 16, "pct": 0.01, "type": "pct"}, "tp": {"type": "none"}, "trail_at": 1.5, "trail_atr": 2.5, "triggers": [{"n": 24, "type": "donchian_break"}]}` exec `{"entry_fill_ratio": 1.0, "entry_mode": "post_only", "entry_offset_atr": 0.1}`

### 087d112106 — 023_trend_regime_long (BTCUSDT 60m) — **PASS**

- 2026-09-29 01:12 UTC · idea `ideas\023_trend_regime_long.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Exp 017 showed that every long entry this project has tried earns about the same as a random long entry inside the same filters, so the interesting question is not WHEN to buy but WHETHER TO BE IN THE MARKET AT ALL. Being long only while BTC trades above its own multi-day trend should keep the compounding during the regimes that carry it and be flat through the ones that do not: buy & hold lost 64% in 2022 and 76.7% peak-to-trough over 2020-22, and a large part of that is unbroken drift below the trend rather than any single crash. The claim is deliberately modest - this is not an alpha claim, it is that the same return arrives with a fraction of the drawdown, which is a HIGHER SHARPE, not a higher CAGR, because 1% risk sizing with a 10% stop keeps only ~10% of the account in BTC and a small beta caps CAGR by construction. The engine cannot exit when the regime ends, so max_hold_hours is a re-check interval and each re-entry pays a real round trip; that cost is 0.009 R at this stop width, cheap enough for the rule to be honest about it.
- grid: 6 combos, 6 with ≥100 train trades, 100% of those positive on train
- chosen params: `{"atr_n": 14, "cooldown_bars": 0, "direction": "long", "filters": [], "max_hold_hours": 72, "stop": {"max_atr": 12.0, "min_atr": 1.5, "pct": 0.1, "type": "pct"}, "tp": {"type": "none"}, "trail_at": 0, "trail_atr": 0, "triggers": [{"n": 100, "type": "trend_state"}]}` exec `{"entry_fill_ratio": 1.0, "entry_mode": "taker", "entry_offset_atr": 0.0}`

### 3cb722efe4 — 025_trend_regime_adx (BTCUSDT 60m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 01:12 UTC · idea `ideas\025_trend_regime_adx.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A close-versus-EMA regime rule is a price rule, and price rules fail in exactly one market: the one with no direction. While BTC oscillates around a 200-hour EMA the rule flips on every re-check interval, pays a round trip each flip, and ends up long or short at random - the whipsaw that gives back the drawdown the rule was built to avoid. ADX is the cheapest available measure of whether there is a direction at all, so requiring ADX above 20-25 should keep the rule out of the regime while it is chop. The cost of that filter is time out of the market during the early part of every new trend, which is the part that does the most work, so this idea is a genuine trade-off and not an obvious improvement: it is worth running precisely because the honest expectation is that it cuts drawdown and costs alpha.

### b7cde6ca70 — 026_trend_regime_vol_filter (BTCUSDT 60m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 01:12 UTC · idea `ideas\026_trend_regime_vol_filter.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The other way a trend regime rule fails is not chop but panic: a close below a 200-hour EMA during a volatility spike is a liquidation cascade, and re-enting short or exiting long into one is how a drawdown gets bigger rather than smaller. Volatility spikes cluster at the moment of maximum loss for a long holder, so being flat while the fast ATR is an extreme multiple of the slow ATR should remove the worst tail of the distribution without giving up the compounding. The honest cost is that volatility is also elevated in the early stages of large genuine moves, so this filter is late by construction; the question is whether the tail it removes is bigger than the trend it misses. It is also the cheapest possible filter to state and the easiest to falsify, which is why it is worth a slot: if it does nothing, the regime rule's drawdown is not coming from panic bars.
- grid: 6 combos, 6 with ≥100 train trades, 100% of those positive on train

### ff51c4e647 — 027_multiday_pullback_long (BTCUSDT 60m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 01:12 UTC · idea `ideas\027_multiday_pullback_long.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long entry this project has tested on 15m-30m was shown to be drift: random long entries inside the same filters earned the same (Exp 017), and the one PASS was killed by the holdout. Those were all short-horizon entries. This is the one long entry idea the evidence has not closed, because it is not short-horizon: a dip to the fast EMA inside a 200-hour up-regime, held for two to four days, is timed rather than continuous, so the counterparty is specific - the seller who is late to a rising market and is hitting bids into a pullback that trend money wants to accumulate. Holding for days also makes cost_r nearly irrelevant, which is the only regime where this project's cost problem has ever been absent: at a 4% stop the round trip is 0.022 R, against 0.106 R for the 1% stop that produced Round 2's false PASS. Judged by both controls, because a long idea in a bull market can pass either one alone.

### 31e7126955 — 023_trend_regime_long_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 01:12 UTC · idea `ideas\023_trend_regime_long_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Exp 017 showed that every long entry this project has tried earns about the same as a random long entry inside the same filters, so the interesting question is not WHEN to buy but WHETHER TO BE IN THE MARKET AT ALL. Being long only while BTC trades above its own multi-day trend should keep the compounding during the regimes that carry it and be flat through the ones that do not: buy & hold lost 64% in 2022 and 76.7% peak-to-trough over 2020-22, and a large part of that is unbroken drift below the trend rather than any single crash. The claim is deliberately modest - this is not an alpha claim, it is that the same return arrives with a fraction of the drawdown, which is a HIGHER SHARPE, not a higher CAGR, because 1% risk sizing with a 10% stop keeps only ~10% of the account in BTC and a small beta caps CAGR by construction. The engine cannot exit when the regime ends, so max_hold_hours is a re-check interval and each re-entry pays a real round trip; that cost is 0.009 R at this stop width, cheap enough for the rule to be honest about it. [Timeframe variant of 023_trend_regime_long, 60m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.5, pct stop x0.707.]

### af65d82f6c — 025_trend_regime_adx_tf240 (BTCUSDT 240m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 01:13 UTC · idea `ideas\025_trend_regime_adx_tf240.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A close-versus-EMA regime rule is a price rule, and price rules fail in exactly one market: the one with no direction. While BTC oscillates around a 200-hour EMA the rule flips on every re-check interval, pays a round trip each flip, and ends up long or short at random - the whipsaw that gives back the drawdown the rule was built to avoid. ADX is the cheapest available measure of whether there is a direction at all, so requiring ADX above 20-25 should keep the rule out of the regime while it is chop. The cost of that filter is time out of the market during the early part of every new trend, which is the part that does the most work, so this idea is a genuine trade-off and not an obvious improvement: it is worth running precisely because the honest expectation is that it cuts drawdown and costs alpha. ### e5f7dc5947 — 026_trend_regime_vol_filter_tf240 (BTCUSDT 240m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 01:13 UTC · idea `ideas\026_trend_regime_vol_filter_tf240.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The other way a trend regime rule fails is not chop but panic: a close below a 200-hour EMA during a volatility spike is a liquidation cascade, and re-enting short or exiting long into one is how a drawdown gets bigger rather than smaller. Volatility spikes cluster at the moment of maximum loss for a long holder, so being flat while the fast ATR is an extreme multiple of the slow ATR should remove the worst tail of the distribution without giving up the compounding. The honest cost is that volatility is also elevated in the early stages of large genuine moves, so this filter is late by construction; the question is whether the tail it removes is bigger than the trend it misses. It is also the cheapest possible filter to state and the easiest to falsify, which is why it is worth a slot: if it does nothing, the regime rule's drawdown is not coming from panic bars. ### a92e8f27cd — 027_multiday_pullback_long_tf240 (BTCUSDT 240m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 01:13 UTC · idea `ideas\027_multiday_pullback_long_tf240.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long entry this project has tested on 15m-30m was shown to be drift: random long entries inside the same filters earned the same (Exp 017), and the one PASS was killed by the holdout. Those were all short-horizon entries. This is the one long entry idea the evidence has not closed, because it is not short-horizon: a dip to the fast EMA inside a 200-hour up-regime, held for two to four days, is timed rather than continuous, so the counterparty is specific - the seller who is late to a rising market and is hitting bids into a pullback that trend money wants to accumulate. Holding for days also makes cost_r nearly irrelevant, which is the only regime where this project's cost problem has ever been absent: at a 4% stop the round trip is 0.022 R, against 0.106 R for the 1% stop that produced Round 2's false PASS. Judged by both controls, because a long idea in a bull market can pass either one alone. [Timeframe variant of 027_multiday_pullback_long, 60m -> 240m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x4, pct stop x2.000.]

### 9f3a74460e — 023_trend_regime_long_tf15 (BTCUSDT 15m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 01:13 UTC · idea `ideas\023_trend_regime_long_tf15.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Exp 017 showed that every long entry this project has tried earns about the same as a random long entry inside the same filters, so the interesting question is not WHEN to buy but WHETHER TO BE IN THE MARKET AT ALL. Being long only while BTC trades above its own multi-day trend should keep the compounding during the regimes that carry it and be flat through the ones that do not: buy & hold lost 64% in 2022 and 76.7% peak-to-trough over 2020-22, and a large part of that is unbroken drift below the trend rather than any single crash. The claim is deliberately modest - this is not an alpha claim, it is that the same return arrives with a fraction of the drawdown, which is a HIGHER SHARPE, not a higher CAGR, because 1% risk sizing with a 10% stop keeps only ~10% of the account in BTC and a small beta caps CAGR by construction. The engine cannot exit when the regime ends, so max_hold_hours is a re-check interval and each re-entry pays a real round trip; that cost is 0.009 R at this stop width, cheap enough for the rule to be honest about it. [Timeframe variant of 023_trend_regime_long, 60m -> 15m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.25, pct stop x0.500.]

### 43188f7471 — 025_trend_regime_adx_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:13 UTC · idea `ideas\025_trend_regime_adx_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A close-versus-EMA regime rule is a price rule, and price rules fail in exactly one market: the one with no direction. While BTC oscillates around a 200-hour EMA the rule flips on every re-check interval, pays a round trip each flip, and ends up long or short at random - the whipsaw that gives back the drawdown the rule was built to avoid. ADX is the cheapest available measure of whether there is a direction at all, so requiring ADX above 20-25 should keep the rule out of the regime while it is chop. The cost of that filter is time out of the market during the early part of every new trend, which is the part that does the most work, so this idea is a genuine trade-off and not an obvious improvement: it is worth running precisely because the honest expectation is that it cuts drawdown and costs alpha. [Timeframe variant of 025_trend_regime_adx, 60m -> 5m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.0833333, pct stop x0.289.]

### 8bf7939f09 — 026_trend_regime_vol_filter_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:13 UTC · idea `ideas\026_trend_regime_vol_filter_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The other way a trend regime rule fails is not chop but panic: a close below a 200-hour EMA during a volatility spike is a liquidation cascade, and re-enting short or exiting long into one is how a drawdown gets bigger rather than smaller. Volatility spikes cluster at the moment of maximum loss for a long holder, so being flat while the fast ATR is an extreme multiple of the slow ATR should remove the worst tail of the distribution without giving up the compounding. The honest cost is that volatility is also elevated in the early stages of large genuine moves, so this filter is late by construction; the question is whether the tail it removes is bigger than the trend it misses. It is also the cheapest possible filter to state and the easiest to falsify, which is why it is worth a slot: if it does nothing, the regime rule's drawdown is not coming from panic bars. [Timeframe variant of 026_trend_regime_vol_filter, 60m -> 5m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.0833333, pct stop x0.289.]

### a1c735ecaa — 027_multiday_pullback_long_tf5 (BTCUSDT 5m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:14 UTC · idea `ideas\027_multiday_pullback_long_tf5.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Every long entry this project has tested on 15m-30m was shown to be drift: random long entries inside the same filters earned the same (Exp 017), and the one PASS was killed by the holdout. Those were all short-horizon entries. This is the one long entry idea the evidence has not closed, because it is not short-horizon: a dip to the fast EMA inside a 200-hour up-regime, held for two to four days, is timed rather than continuous, so the counterparty is specific - the seller who is late to a rising market and is hitting bids into a pullback that trend money wants to accumulate. Holding for days also makes cost_r nearly irrelevant, which is the only regime where this project's cost problem has ever been absent: at a 4% stop the round trip is 0.022 R, against 0.106 R for the 1% stop that produced Round 2's false PASS. Judged by both controls, because a long idea in a bull market can pass either one alone. [Timeframe variant of 027_multiday_pullback_long, 60m -> 5m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.0833333, pct stop x0.289.]

### db80be48e1 — 023_trend_regime_long_tf1 (BTCUSDT 1m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:15 UTC · idea `ideas\023_trend_regime_long_tf1.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Exp 017 showed that every long entry this project has tried earns about the same as a random long entry inside the same filters, so the interesting question is not WHEN to buy but WHETHER TO BE IN THE MARKET AT ALL. Being long only while BTC trades above its own multi-day trend should keep the compounding during the regimes that carry it and be flat through the ones that do not: buy & hold lost 64% in 2022 and 76.7% peak-to-trough over 2020-22, and a large part of that is unbroken drift below the trend rather than any single crash. The claim is deliberately modest - this is not an alpha claim, it is that the same return arrives with a fraction of the drawdown, which is a HIGHER SHARPE, not a higher CAGR, because 1% risk sizing with a 10% stop keeps only ~10% of the account in BTC and a small beta caps CAGR by construction. The engine cannot exit when the regime ends, so max_hold_hours is a re-check interval and each re-entry pays a real round trip; that cost is 0.009 R at this stop width, cheap enough for the rule to be honest about it. ### 1208c561f3 — 025_trend_regime_adx_tf1 (BTCUSDT 1m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:16 UTC · idea `ideas\025_trend_regime_adx_tf1.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A close-versus-EMA regime rule is a price rule, and price rules fail in exactly one market: the one with no direction. While BTC oscillates around a 200-hour EMA the rule flips on every re-check interval, pays a round trip each flip, and ends up long or short at random - the whipsaw that gives back the drawdown the rule was built to avoid. ADX is the cheapest available measure of whether there is a direction at all, so requiring ADX above 20-25 should keep the rule out of the regime while it is chop. The cost of that filter is time out of the market during the early part of every new trend, which is the part that does the most work, so this idea is a genuine trade-off and not an obvious improvement: it is worth running precisely because the honest expectation is that it cuts drawdown and costs alpha. The honest cost is that volatility is also elevated in the early stages of large genuine moves, so this filter is late by construction; the question is whether the tail it removes is bigger than the trend it misses. ### f981e4dc41 — 028_prev_day_break (BTCUSDT 60m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\028_prev_day_break.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Yesterday's high and low are the two levels on this market that other people actually use. Retail and desk stops sit just beyond them, breakout orders are queued at them, and the daily risk reports measure distance to them, so a break of either one is where resting supply and forced risk-reduction meet. The counterparty here is concrete and identifiable - it is the stop order, not a mood - and a stop that is triggered on the way through a level tends to be followed, because the orders behind it are filled at the worst moment for the person who placed them. This is the one entry in the project whose claimed mechanism does not require BTC to go anywhere in particular, which is why it survives the Round 3 finding that every directional entry was drift.

### 6f8721cffc — 029_opening_range (BTCUSDT 60m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\029_opening_range.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The 00:00 UTC print resets positioning once a day. Every leveraged account, every market maker and every volatility seller rebuilds its brackets around that one print, and the first hour after it is the window in which they agree on a reference price. Once that hour is over, the first close outside it is the first moment where those brackets are all on the wrong side of price in the same direction, and the day's direction is decided by whoever is first forced to cover. This is a market-structure claim rather than a directional one: it says the 00:00 range is a level with order flow behind it, which is testable without assuming where BTC is going.

### 619a60427d — 030_funding_window (BTCUSDT 60m) — **REJECT**

- hypothesis: Funding settles every eight hours, and around each settlement there is a mechanical flow that exists at no other time: positions that want to collect the funding have to be open and sized before the timestamp, so they are opened into the window and closed out of it, and the ones that would rather not pay the cost are closed and reopened. ### a5e920e6a0 — 031_liquidation_flush (BTCUSDT 60m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\031_liquidation_flush.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A bar whose range is twice the ATR while its volume is one and a half times its own average is a liquidation cascade, and the two possible readings of that event are exact opposites, so the grid is what chooses between them. FADE: the cascade is forced selling into thin books, the overshoot is liquidity that has to be replenished, and price snaps back to where it was. FOLLOW: the cascade is the point at which leverage is removed, and the market trends once the liquid accounts are out. Which one is true is not knowable in advance, so the mode is a grid key rather than an opinion: if neither side has a positive gross_r then the event has no exploitable side at all and the whole family of 'liquidation' strategies is dead. Both modes are tested with direction both, so the sign of the cascade is not assumed either.

### 12aa8c807b — 032_keltner_break (BTCUSDT 60m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\032_keltner_break.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A Donchian channel is a price channel: its width is the range of the last n bars and it changes only when price makes a new extreme, so in a quiet regime the channel sits far from price and a 'break' is often just a move back inside the range it left. A Keltner channel is a volatility channel: EMA(n) plus a multiple of ATR, so it widens when volatility rises and narrows when it falls, and a break of it means price has left a band whose width is itself a measure of how far price has been moving. If false breakouts are what killed the Donchian versions in Rounds 1-2, then the same entry with a volatility-scaled band should have a higher gross_r, and that is the whole claim - the entry is otherwise identical, so the comparison isolates the band.

### b4fb859a13 — 028_prev_day_break_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\028_prev_day_break_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Yesterday's high and low are the two levels on this market that other people actually use. Retail and desk stops sit just beyond them, breakout orders are queued at them, and the daily risk reports measure distance to them, so a break of either one is where resting supply and forced risk-reduction meet. The counterparty here is concrete and identifiable - it is the stop order, not a mood - and a stop that is triggered on the way through a level tends to be followed, because the orders behind it are filled at the worst moment for the person who placed them. This is the one entry in the project whose claimed mechanism does not require BTC to go anywhere in particular, which is why it survives the Round 3 finding that every directional entry was drift. ### d9f3382e2c — 029_opening_range_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\029_opening_range_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The 00:00 UTC print resets positioning once a day. Every leveraged account, every market maker and every volatility seller rebuilds its brackets around that one print, and the first hour after it is the window in which they agree on a reference price. Once that hour is over, the first close outside it is the first moment where those brackets are all on the wrong side of price in the same direction, and the day's direction is decided by whoever is first forced to cover. This is a market-structure claim rather than a directional one: it says the 00:00 range is a level with order flow behind it, which is testable without assuming where BTC is going. [Timeframe variant of 029_opening_range, 60m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.5, pct stop x0.707.]

### 1ed66f0172 — 030_funding_window_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\030_funding_window_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Funding settles every eight hours, and around each settlement there is a mechanical flow that exists at no other time: positions that want to collect the funding have to be open and sized before the timestamp, so they are opened into the window and closed out of it, and the ones that would rather not pay the cost are closed and reopened. That is a flow with a known clock, so it is a TIMING claim, not a directional one, which is exactly what the project needs after three rounds of entries that turned out to be drift. The claim is that a market-structure entry taken inside the window behaves differently from the same entry taken outside it, and the test is whether adding the window to R4.1's entry changes its result. ### dc9bae9493 — 031_liquidation_flush_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\031_liquidation_flush_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A bar whose range is twice the ATR while its volume is one and a half times its own average is a liquidation cascade, and the two possible readings of that event are exact opposites, so the grid is what chooses between them. FADE: the cascade is forced selling into thin books, the overshoot is liquidity that has to be replenished, and price snaps back to where it was. FOLLOW: the cascade is the point at which leverage is removed, and the market trends once the liquid accounts are out. Which one is true is not knowable in advance, so the mode is a grid key rather than an opinion: if neither side has a positive gross_r then the event has no exploitable side at all and the whole family of 'liquidation' strategies is dead. Both modes are tested with direction both, so the sign of the cascade is not assumed either. [Timeframe variant of 031_liquidation_flush, 60m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.5, pct stop x0.707.]

### 633492e1e1 — 032_keltner_break_tf30 (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\032_keltner_break_tf30.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A Donchian channel is a price channel: its width is the range of the last n bars and it changes only when price makes a new extreme, so in a quiet regime the channel sits far from price and a 'break' is often just a move back inside the range it left. A Keltner channel is a volatility channel: EMA(n) plus a multiple of ATR, so it widens when volatility rises and narrows when it falls, and a break of it means price has left a band whose width is itself a measure of how far price has been moving. If false breakouts are what killed the Donchian versions in Rounds 1-2, then the same entry with a volatility-scaled band should have a higher gross_r, and that is the whole claim - the entry is otherwise identical, so the comparison isolates the band. [Timeframe variant of 032_keltner_break, 60m -> 30m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.5, pct stop x0.707.]
- grid: 6 combos, 6 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\028_prev_day_break_tf15.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Yesterday's high and low are the two levels on this market that other people actually use. Retail and desk stops sit just beyond them, breakout orders are queued at them, and the daily risk reports measure distance to them, so a break of either one is where resting supply and forced risk-reduction meet. ### 00bd226f05 — 029_opening_range_tf15 (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:50 UTC · idea `ideas\029_opening_range_tf15.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The 00:00 UTC print resets positioning once a day. Every leveraged account, every market maker and every volatility seller rebuilds its brackets around that one print, and the first hour after it is the window in which they agree on a reference price. Once that hour is over, the first close outside it is the first moment where those brackets are all on the wrong side of price in the same direction, and the day's direction is decided by whoever is first forced to cover. This is a market-structure claim rather than a directional one: it says the 00:00 range is a level with order flow behind it, which is testable without assuming where BTC is going. [Timeframe variant of 029_opening_range, 60m -> 15m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.25, pct stop x0.500.]

### 1fee8c915d — 030_funding_window_tf15 (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:51 UTC · idea `ideas\030_funding_window_tf15.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Funding settles every eight hours, and around each settlement there is a mechanical flow that exists at no other time: positions that want to collect the funding have to be open and sized before the timestamp, so they are opened into the window and closed out of it, and the ones that would rather not pay the cost are closed and reopened. That is a flow with a known clock, so it is a TIMING claim, not a directional one, which is exactly what the project needs after three rounds of entries that turned out to be drift. The claim is that a market-structure entry taken inside the window behaves differently from the same entry taken outside it, and the test is whether adding the window to R4.1's entry changes its result. [Timeframe variant of 030_funding_window, 60m -> 15m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.25, pct stop x0.500.]

### 2477052481 — 031_liquidation_flush_tf15 (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:51 UTC · idea `ideas\031_liquidation_flush_tf15.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A bar whose range is twice the ATR while its volume is one and a half times its own average is a liquidation cascade, and the two possible readings of that event are exact opposites, so the grid is what chooses between them. FADE: the cascade is forced selling into thin books, the overshoot is liquidity that has to be replenished, and price snaps back to where it was. FOLLOW: the cascade is the point at which leverage is removed, and the market trends once the liquid accounts are out. Which one is true is not knowable in advance, so the mode is a grid key rather than an opinion: if neither side has a positive gross_r then the event has no exploitable side at all and the whole family of 'liquidation' strategies is dead. Both modes are tested with direction both, so the sign of the cascade is not assumed either. [Timeframe variant of 031_liquidation_flush, 60m -> 15m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.25, pct stop x0.500.]

### 93bd32b6e1 — 032_keltner_break_tf15 (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:51 UTC · idea `ideas\032_keltner_break_tf15.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A Donchian channel is a price channel: its width is the range of the last n bars and it changes only when price makes a new extreme, so in a quiet regime the channel sits far from price and a 'break' is often just a move back inside the range it left. A Keltner channel is a volatility channel: EMA(n) plus a multiple of ATR, so it widens when volatility rises and narrows when it falls, and a break of it means price has left a band whose width is itself a measure of how far price has been moving. If false breakouts are what killed the Donchian versions in Rounds 1-2, then the same entry with a volatility-scaled band should have a higher gross_r, and that is the whole claim - the entry is otherwise identical, so the comparison isolates the band. [Timeframe variant of 032_keltner_break, 60m -> 15m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.25, pct stop x0.500.]
- grid: 6 combos, 6 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### eeaa9315f5 — 029_opening_range_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:52 UTC · idea `ideas\029_opening_range_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: The 00:00 UTC print resets positioning once a day. Every leveraged account, every market maker and every volatility seller rebuilds its brackets around that one print, and the first hour after it is the window in which they agree on a reference price. Once that hour is over, the first close outside it is the first moment where those brackets are all on the wrong side of price in the same direction, and the day's direction is decided by whoever is first forced to cover. This is a market-structure claim rather than a directional one: it says the 00:00 range is a level with order flow behind it, which is testable without assuming where BTC is going. ### 2cdc7c4aba — 030_funding_window_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:52 UTC · idea `ideas\030_funding_window_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Funding settles every eight hours, and around each settlement there is a mechanical flow that exists at no other time: positions that want to collect the funding have to be open and sized before the timestamp, so they are opened into the window and closed out of it, and the ones that would rather not pay the cost are closed and reopened. That is a flow with a known clock, so it is a TIMING claim, not a directional one, which is exactly what the project needs after three rounds of entries that turned out to be drift. The claim is that a market-structure entry taken inside the window behaves differently from the same entry taken outside it, and the test is whether adding the window to R4.1's entry changes its result. ### d7a79484d3 — 031_liquidation_flush_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:52 UTC · idea `ideas\031_liquidation_flush_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A bar whose range is twice the ATR while its volume is one and a half times its own average is a liquidation cascade, and the two possible readings of that event are exact opposites, so the grid is what chooses between them. FADE: the cascade is forced selling into thin books, the overshoot is liquidity that has to be replenished, and price snaps back to where it was. FOLLOW: the cascade is the point at which leverage is removed, and the market trends once the liquid accounts are out. Which one is true is not knowable in advance, so the mode is a grid key rather than an opinion: if neither side has a positive gross_r then the event has no exploitable side at all and the whole family of 'liquidation' strategies is dead. Both modes are tested with direction both, so the sign of the cascade is not assumed either. [Timeframe variant of 031_liquidation_flush, 60m -> 3m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.05, pct stop x0.224.]

### 7e59da57b0 — 032_keltner_break_tf3 (BTCUSDT 3m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 01:53 UTC · idea `ideas\032_keltner_break_tf3.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A Donchian channel is a price channel: its width is the range of the last n bars and it changes only when price makes a new extreme, so in a quiet regime the channel sits far from price and a 'break' is often just a move back inside the range it left. A Keltner channel is a volatility channel: EMA(n) plus a multiple of ATR, so it widens when volatility rises and narrows when it falls, and a break of it means price has left a band whose width is itself a measure of how far price has been moving. If false breakouts are what killed the Donchian versions in Rounds 1-2, then the same entry with a volatility-scaled band should have a higher gross_r, and that is the whole claim - the entry is otherwise identical, so the comparison isolates the band. [Timeframe variant of 032_keltner_break, 60m -> 3m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.05, pct stop x0.224.]

If false breakouts are what killed the Donchian versions in Rounds 1-2, then the same entry with a volatility-scaled band should have a higher gross_r, and that is the whole claim - the entry is otherwise identical, so the comparison isolates the band. [Timeframe variant of 032_keltner_break, 60m -> 1m, mode chart: same bar counts and ATR multiples, hold x0.0166667, pct stop x0.129.]
- grid: 6 combos, 6 with ≥100 train trades, 0% of those positive on train

### b9834ca404 — 033_funding_carry (BTCUSDT 240m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\033_funding_carry.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Funding is not a fee, it is a transfer: every eight hours the longs pay the shorts, at a rate set by the imbalance between the two sides' leverage. When the rate is extreme, the paying side is the crowded side, and the reason it pays is that its positions are too big to unwind at once. That is the mechanism this idea trades: not a price reversal but a position-size imbalance, whose unwinding takes days rather than hours. Take the paying side's side - short when longs are paying - and the position earns the funding while it waits. Idea 003 tried this at 15m with a 12h hold and produced 13 trades, so the hypothesis has never really been tested at a horizon where the unwind can happen. Short-only in practice on this sample (154 TRAIN signals, 108 of them short), and the long/short split is reported separately because a carry idea that is secretly one-directional is a drift idea.
- grid: 4 combos, 2 with ≥100 train trades, 100% of those positive on train

### ef1d7e1355 — 034_multiday_reversal (BTCUSDT 240m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\034_multiday_reversal.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Over five to ten days a 2-sigma move is not information, it is a position: the move is long enough that trend followers have added to it and leveraged accounts have been funded into it, and both need out at the same time. The counterparty is therefore not a view but a margin call, and margin calls unwind on the timeframe of days, not hours - which is why this was never visible at the 12h horizons where mean reversion was tested and rejected. The edge, if there is one, is that a stretched move overshoots because forced selling does not stop at a fair price, and part of the overshoot is handed back as the forced sellers become the buyers. This is the one reversion idea the project has not closed, and the difference from Exp 016's is the horizon, not the signal.

### 9c6deeabb3 — 035_multiday_momentum (BTCUSDT 240m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\035_multiday_momentum.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A five to twenty day high or low is where trend-following funds add risk, because their mandate is to hold the extreme rather than to forecast it. That is a structural reason for the move to continue rather than reverse: the buyers of a new high are institutions with a rule that says buy, not people with a view. The reason to test it both-sided with no directional filter is that this effect should not care about the sign of the market - it continued down through 2022 and up through 2023-24, and a rule that only works in the bull market is the drift this project has spent four rounds trying to separate from an edge. The kill condition is deliberately two-sided: it dies if TRAIN gross_r <= 0, and it also dies if the whole result is one side in the period where that side matches the drift.

### 5d76a49559 — 036_keltner_multiday (BTCUSDT 240m) — **WATCH**

### 15aa08599f — 037_turn_of_month_fade (BTCUSDT 240m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\037_turn_of_month_fade.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Month-end and month-start are calendar events, not price events. Institutional risk budgets reset, index products and mandates rebalance, and benchmark-tracking funds must sell what fell and buy what rose in order to return to their weights - all of it in the last days of a month and the first days of the next, without any view on BTC. That creates a directional push that ends on a date, which is a testable asymmetry rather than a mood: the flow that pushed price into the month boundary is the flow that stops at it. So this idea fades the move that runs into a month boundary and is a natural both-sided trade - it is long after a drop into the window and short after a rise into it, which is why it is the only idea left that is structurally incapable of being a disguised long, and why the round's beta requirement is the real test of it. The counterparty is the rebalancing flow itself, and it is calendar-driven, so the effect should be present in every year rather than tracking the market's direction.

### 6137281ca4 — 038_opening_range_both_sides (BTCUSDT 240m) — **PASS**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\038_opening_range_both_sides.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A bug correction, not a new hypothesis. R4.2's 00:00 UTC opening-range break returned a (long, short) tuple where every trigger returns one signed array, so `recipe()` read 'either side fired' as LONG and the whole of Exp 022 ran long-only: 798 long trades and 0 short. Its control verdicts (DRIFT, NO_EDGE) stand and the round's conclusion is unchanged, but the structure itself was never actually tested on both sides, and it was the only Round 4 structure whose gross move exceeded the 0.11% cost line (+0.141% of price at 4h). The 00:00 print resets positioning once a day - brackets are rebuilt around it - so its first hour is a level with order flow behind it, and a close outside it is the first moment those brackets are all on the wrong side. This re-test adds what Round 4 did not have: a 6% stop and a 96h hold, so the gross move is measured against a cost of 0.043 R rather than 0.15 R.

### d64d5cceb5 — 038_opening_range_both_sides_tf60_time (BTCUSDT 60m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\038_opening_range_both_sides_tf60_time.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A bug correction, not a new hypothesis. R4.2's 00:00 UTC opening-range break returned a (long, short) tuple where every trigger returns one signed array, so `recipe()` read 'either side fired' as LONG and the whole of Exp 022 ran long-only: 798 long trades and 0 short. This re-test adds what Round 4 did not have: a 6% stop and a 96h hold, so the gross move is measured against a cost of 0.043 R rather than 0.15 R. ### 5b08933c09 — 033_funding_carry_tf30_time (BTCUSDT 30m) — **INCONCLUSIVE**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\033_funding_carry_tf30_time.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Funding is not a fee, it is a transfer: every eight hours the longs pay the shorts, at a rate set by the imbalance between the two sides' leverage. When the rate is extreme, the paying side is the crowded side, and the reason it pays is that its positions are too big to unwind at once. That is the mechanism this idea trades: not a price reversal but a position-size imbalance, whose unwinding takes days rather than hours. Take the paying side's side - short when longs are paying - and the position earns the funding while it waits. Idea 003 tried this at 15m with a 12h hold and produced 13 trades, so the hypothesis has never really been tested at a horizon where the unwind can happen. Short-only in practice on this sample (154 TRAIN signals, 108 of them short), and the long/short split is reported separately because a carry idea that is secretly one-directional is a drift idea. [Timeframe variant of 033_funding_carry, 240m -> 30m, mode time: bar counts x8, ATR multiples x2.828, same hours and % stops.]

### e248b84bd2 — 034_multiday_reversal_tf30_time (BTCUSDT 30m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\034_multiday_reversal_tf30_time.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Over five to ten days a 2-sigma move is not information, it is a position: the move is long enough that trend followers have added to it and leveraged accounts have been funded into it, and both need out at the same time. The counterparty is therefore not a view but a margin call, and margin calls unwind on the timeframe of days, not hours - which is why this was never visible at the 12h horizons where mean reversion was tested and rejected. The edge, if there is one, is that a stretched move overshoots because forced selling does not stop at a fair price, and part of the overshoot is handed back as the forced sellers become the buyers. This is the one reversion idea the project has not closed, and the difference from Exp 016's is the horizon, not the signal. [Timeframe variant of 034_multiday_reversal, 240m -> 30m, mode time: bar counts x8, ATR multiples x2.828, same hours and % stops.]

### ce1e41ab72 — 035_multiday_momentum_tf30_time (BTCUSDT 30m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\035_multiday_momentum_tf30_time.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A five to twenty day high or low is where trend-following funds add risk, because their mandate is to hold the extreme rather than to forecast it. That is a structural reason for the move to continue rather than reverse: the buyers of a new high are institutions with a rule that says buy, not people with a view. The reason to test it both-sided with no directional filter is that this effect should not care about the sign of the market - it continued down through 2022 and up through 2023-24, and a rule that only works in the bull market is the drift this project has spent four rounds trying to separate from an edge. The kill condition is deliberately two-sided: it dies if TRAIN gross_r <= 0, and it also dies if the whole result is one side in the period where that side matches the drift. ### d4f47d196c — 035_multiday_momentum_tf15_time (BTCUSDT 15m) — **WATCH**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\035_multiday_momentum_tf15_time.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: A five to twenty day high or low is where trend-following funds add risk, because their mandate is to hold the extreme rather than to forecast it. That is a structural reason for the move to continue rather than reverse: the buyers of a new high are institutions with a rule that says buy, not people with a view. The reason to test it both-sided with no directional filter is that this effect should not care about the sign of the market - it continued down through 2022 and up through 2023-24, and a rule that only works in the bull market is the drift this project has spent four rounds trying to separate from an edge. The kill condition is deliberately two-sided: it dies if TRAIN gross_r <= 0, and it also dies if the whole result is one side in the period where that side matches the drift. ### 10268f2847 — 036_keltner_multiday_tf15_time (BTCUSDT 15m) — **REJECT**

- 2026-09-29 02:19 UTC · idea `ideas\036_keltner_multiday_tf15_time.json` · strategy `recipe`
- hypothesis: Idea 032 put a Keltner break on a 12h hold with an htf_trend filter, and produced Round 4's largest TRAIN gross - and was DRIFT, which is the pattern of the whole round: a real move at a horizon where cost eats it, or a filter that makes a both-sided idea long. This idea removes both problems at once. The channel is a volatility band, so it widens when price has been moving and narrows when it has not, and its break therefore means price has left a band whose width is itself a measure of movement. Held for days with a 6% stop, the gross move it captures is several times the 0.11% cost line, and with no directional filter at all it cannot be a disguised long. The pairs gridded are the two that survive constraint 5: `mult` is fixed at 2.0 because (n 20, mult 3.0) produced only 62 TRAIN signals and was dropped before running.
